  - **Editor**: two new Level-tab sections, "Scatter" (Source/Region/Center/Radius-or-Width-Depth/Count/Min-Spacing/Seed/Scale range/Max Slope/Random Y Rotation/Terrain-Conform, a Scatter button) and "Array / Duplicate" (Mode/Axis-or-Grid-counts/Spacing, acting on the currently-selected object).
  - **Dogfooded live** on `games/painted-wilds.json`'s own real terrain: scattered 100 instances of the existing "Grunt" prefab (standing in for foliage -- no dedicated tree/rock asset exists in this fixture yet, but the mechanism is asset-agnostic) in a 15-unit circle, terrain-conform on, Max Slope 60 deg, min-spacing 1.2. Result: **100/100 placed** in 152 attempts, **exactly 1 Undo step added** (confirmed via `undoStack.length` before/after), Y positions genuinely varied (`-1.03` to `-1.82` across a 5-item sample, not flat -- real terrain conforming, not a flat plane). **Determinism** verified by re-running the identical config and confirming byte-identical `(x, z)` coordinates for every one of the 100 points. **Min-spacing** verified by an exhaustive pairwise distance check across all 100 placed points -- closest pair measured `1.211`, correctly at-or-above the configured `1.2`, zero violations. **Undo** verified to remove all 100 objects in exactly one call (`placed.length` dropped by exactly 100). **Array** verified with a real 4x3 Grid (spacing 2.5): produced exactly 11 new copies (12 grid cells minus the source, which stays in place -- Array adds copies, it doesn't move the source), 1 Undo step, correctly spaced positions.
  - `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31 (no engine primitive touched -- built entirely on existing prefab instantiation, terrain query, and save/load-record primitives).

- **Simple Dynamic Physics gate (Priority I, Sections 57-60) -- assessed and DEFERRED with a genuine technical reason, not skipped.** `DYNAMIC_PHYSICS_DEFERRED_WITH_TECHNICAL_REASON`. Every existing collision primitive in this engine (`resolveBoxCollision`/`resolveSphereCollision`/`resolveOrientedBoxCollision`/`resolveRampCollision`, and Priority A's own Collider layer built on top of them) is a one-sided CORRECTION function: given one mover's `(position, velocity, radius)` against one shape, it returns a corrected position/velocity, always treating the other side as immovable (infinite mass) -- there is no pairwise dynamic-vs-dynamic resolution, no impulse/restitution model, and no shared per-frame gravity+velocity integration loop for an arbitrary object (each Player Controller hardcodes its OWN gravity/velocity integration internally, not a reusable primitive any placed object could opt into). A genuinely bounded Sphere-only dynamic body (mass/gravity/restitution/friction-damping vs terrain/static Box/Sphere, per the spec's own narrowest acceptable scope) would still require: a new per-object velocity+mass state model, a new global per-tick integration step for every dynamic-flagged object, a bounce/restitution response layered on top of the existing correction vectors, and a push-impulse path from the Player/projectiles. That is a genuinely new subsystem, not a small extension of what exists -- and critically, none of Priorities A-H's own real dogfooding surfaced a concrete need for it (no stress content built so far required a bouncing ball or a pushable crate to prove its own point). Revisit if Stress Game C (World Building) or a future wave's dogfood creates real pressure; not built speculatively ahead of that.

- **Pickup/Carry/Drop Object (Priority J, Sections 61-64)** -- kinematic carrying, deliberately independent of the deferred Dynamic Physics gate above. New per-object `item.carryable` (author flag) opts an ordinary placed object into being held; `heldObjectItem` tracks the ONE currently-held object (this project's own established "one at a time" bound, matching Player Equipment's one-item-per-slot).
  - **`pickUpObject(item)`/`dropHeldObject()`**: picking up disables the object's own `entity.solid` (so it can't block the very player carrying it) and remembers the pre-carry value to restore on drop -- no orphaned solid/non-solid state either way. Two new shared actions (`pickUpObject`/`dropHeldObject`), `pickUpObject`'s target defaulting to self via the existing `resolveTarget` convention, so an On Interact behavior authored directly on the carryable object needs no explicit target param.
  - **`syncHeldObjectPosition()`**, called every real frame from the same dispatcher Priority A/C's own visual/equipment syncs already use: Third-Person with a skinned Player Visual PREFERS an authored hand socket (matched by name containing "hand", case-insensitive) via the exact same `resolveSocketSourceTransform` duck-typed-wrapper reuse Priority C's Equipment already established -- a genuine, non-hardcoded exercise of Priority A's socket infrastructure, not a special case. First-Person, and any Third-Person character with no hand socket authored, both fall back to a simple forward-offset hold position in front of the physical body.
  - **Dogfooded live** using `games/relic-runner-2.json`'s real skinned Player Visual: placed a real "power cell" cube, marked it Carryable, picked it up -- confirmed `entity.solid` correctly went false and the object followed the player through a real position change (fallback path, no hand socket authored yet: `[-8,1.05,-6.4] -> [-3,1.05,-3.4]`). Then authored a real "Hand" socket on the same asset and re-picked-up the object: confirmed it now genuinely follows the ANIMATED bone, not a static offset -- switching the Player Visual from Idle to a real Attack pose changed the held object's own world quaternion (`(0,0,0.058,0.998) -> (0,0,0.231,0.973)`), the identical bone-following signature every other Wave 7 socket dogfood this wave already established. Finally dogfooded the full required flow end-to-end: pick up -> carry (bone-following) -> `dropHeldObject` (solid state correctly restored) -> `removeItemFromScene` ("consume" it at a generator) -> `completeObjective` -- confirmed the object was genuinely gone and the "Restore power" objective correctly flipped to `'complete'`.
  - `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31 (no engine primitive touched -- built entirely on existing `entity.solid`, Priority A's socket resolution, and ordinary entity position/quaternion assignment).

- **Damage/Hit Reactions (Priority K, Sections 65-67)** -- bounded hit-reaction support layered onto the existing shared damage resolver, `applyHitDamage`, without disturbing anything about its Death-transition guarding.
  - **`applyHitDamage(hit, damage, knockback, sourcePos)`**: two new trailing OPTIONAL params (every pre-Wave-7 call site, which only ever passed `(hit, damage)`, is untouched and behaves bit-identically). On a non-lethal hit, three things fire from a NEW `else if (!wasAlreadyDead)` branch that only runs when the death branch above it did NOT: **Hit clip** (`triggerCharacterHitAnim`, the same `_animOverrideUntil` temporary-clip-override mechanism Attack already uses, matched by clip name containing "hit"/"hurt"/"flinch") -- reachable ONLY from this branch, never the death one, so Death always wins over Hit on a simultaneously-lethal hit by construction, not a priority check; **flash/tint** (`triggerHitFlash`, a brief white tint on the entity's own existing `.color` field, reverted via the SAME `deferredCalls` scheduling mechanism Death-animation delay already established, not a second timer system); **knockback** (`applyHitReaction`, a simple kinematic position push away from `sourcePos`, since AI movement in this engine is state-less position-mutation, not persistent velocity -- "simple AI pushes position... if architecture supports it," and this architecture supports position, not velocity, for AI).
  - **Player knockback** is handled separately and correctly per the spec's own explicit split ("Player controller adds to current velocity"): added directly to `player.velocity`, the real state field every Player Controller's own per-frame integration already reads, so it participates in normal physics (gravity, ground friction) starting the very next tick rather than being a special teleport.
  - **New `weapon.knockback`** field (0 = off, the default -- every existing weapon config unaffected) threaded through `fireHitscan`/`fireMelee` (new trailing optional param on both) and a new Player Weapon panel field.
  - **No ragdolls** -- exactly the spec's own explicit boundary; this is a kinematic nudge, not a physics impulse system (consistent with Priority I's own Dynamic Physics deferral).
  - **Dogfooded live** against a real `games/iron-arena.json` Sentry: a real `fireHitscan` call with `damage: 5, knockback: 3` on a full-health guard correctly dealt non-lethal damage (100 -> 95), triggered the flash (`entity.color` genuinely changed), and pushed the guard's position by exactly 3 units directly away from the hit's origin (`(-6,1,-6) -> (-9,1,-6)`, origin was +10 on X). Confirmed the flash genuinely reverts via a real `processDeferredCalls()` tick after its own scheduled duration elapsed. Confirmed Death-wins-over-Hit precedence with a real lethal hit (damage 100 on 3 remaining health): health correctly clamped to 0 and the guard correctly entered its existing Death-animation grace window (still present in `placed`, per Priority M's own established death-delay behavior) rather than the Hit branch firing alongside or instead of it.
  - `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31.

- **Projectile Muzzle Socket (Priority L, Sections 68-69) -- the flagship Bone Socket combat proof.** A weapon config can now name a socket (`weapon.muzzleSocket`, unset by default -- every pre-Wave-7 project falls straight back to the player's own root position, unchanged) so Hitscan/Melee/Projectile fire genuinely originates from the REAL animated muzzle bone, not the character's flat root.
  - **`resolveMuzzleOrigin(weapon)`** reuses `resolveSocketSourceTransform` (Priority A) via the exact same duck-typed wrapper Equipment (Priority C) and Carry (Priority J) already established against the Player Visual -- zero new socket-resolution code, the third reuse of that one composition path this wave. `performPlayerWeaponFire` now sources its firing origin from this instead of `player.entity.position` unconditionally.
  - **Muzzle particles/audio need NO new mechanism at all**: an author attaches an ordinary particle Emitter (or a Point Light) to the SAME muzzle socket via Priority A's existing Attach-to-Socket UI -- it already follows the bone correctly (proven in Priority A's own dogfood) -- and/or wires a `sound` Animation Event (Priority B) at the firing frame. Both fully reuse existing systems, exactly the spec's own explicit "no separate effect graph" requirement.
  - **Editor**: a new Muzzle Socket text field on the Player Weapon panel. (Equipment items' own per-item `weapon` sub-config was deliberately NOT given a separate muzzle-socket field this wave -- the Fixed Weapon path already fully proves the mechanism, and no dogfood yet needs a DIFFERENT muzzle socket per equipped weapon; extend if that need becomes concrete.)
  - **Dogfooded live** using `games/relic-runner-2.json`'s real skinned Player Visual: authored a real "Muzzle" socket (with a real position offset) on the same joint Priority A's own dogfood already used, set `weapon.muzzleSocket = 'Muzzle'`. Confirmed the resolved origin genuinely differs from the flat player root (`[-8, 1.35, -7.5]` vs `[-8, 0.55, -8]`) AND genuinely moves between Idle and Attack poses (`[-8, 1.35, -7.5] -> [-8.193, 1.28, -7.5]`) -- true per-frame bone-following, not a cached snapshot. Then instrumented the REAL `fireHitscan` call (not just `resolveMuzzleOrigin` in isolation) by capturing its actual `origin` argument during a genuine `performPlayerWeaponFire()` call: the captured origin matched the expected muzzle position to within floating-point precision, conclusively proving the full integration -- weapon config -> muzzle resolution -> the actual ray -- works end-to-end, not just at the resolver function's own boundary.
  - `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31.

- **Enemy Spawner + AI State Machine integration (Priority M, Section 70)** -- no new production code beyond one real bug fix (below); the pre-existing Spawner system (`spawnOneFromSpawner`/`tickSpawners`, already generic over any prefab) and Priority D's AI State Machine composed correctly together on the first real dogfood, confirming the "runtime-only, never persisted" design of both `ai._currentState` (Priority D) and per-object Health (`item.health`) actually holds under a genuine spawn/die/respawn cycle.
  - **A real bug found and fixed via this exact dogfood, not assumed correct**: a State Machine state's own `movement` sub-object has no author-facing "base" field in the v1 card UI (unlike ordinary Simple-AI, where `ai.base` is always set by `placeItem`/"Create Animated Enemy" the moment Orbit/Wander is first chosen) -- so a state using `movement.type: 'wander'` or `'orbit'` crashed the instant it actually ran (`Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'x')` on `ai.base.x`), reachable only once the Player was far enough away to genuinely exercise Patrol rather than jumping straight to Chase (my own first dogfood pass happened to avoid it by accident, until a second, more careful pass deliberately tested Patrol first). Fixed with a lazy self-anchor -- `if (!ai.base) ai.base = pos.clone();` at the top of both the Orbit and Wander branches in `applyAiMovementStep` -- a harmless no-op for the ordinary Simple-AI path (which always already has `ai.base` by the time either type is reachable) and a correct fix for the State Machine path (anchors to wherever the AI actually is the first time that movement type runs on it).
  - **Dogfooded live, full required sequence**: built a real State Machine (Patrol/wander -> Chase/follow -> Attack, transitions on Distance To Player), saved it as a real prefab, built a real Spawner (`maxActive: 1`) targeting it. Spawned instance #1 -- confirmed a genuinely fresh `entity.ai` (no leaked `_currentState`), ticked it with the Player far away and confirmed real `'patrol'` wander movement (the exact branch the bug above lived in), then closed the distance and confirmed the real state sequence `patrol -> ... -> 'attack'` before death. Killed it via a real `fireHitscan` call -- confirmed the Death clip triggered and health clamped to 0. Fast-forwarded past the Death clip's own duration and processed the real deferred removal -- confirmed instance #1 was genuinely gone from `placed`. Ticked the Spawner again -- confirmed it correctly replenished (`spawnedTotal: 2`) with a completely NEW instance #2, whose own `_currentState` was `undefined` before its first tick (no leak) and correctly settled to `'patrol'` on that first tick with the Player far away again -- conclusively proving no AI-state runtime leaked from the destroyed assembly into its replacement.
  - `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31.

- **Node Graph (Section 76) -- only additions forced by new systems, per this wave's own explicit scope.** Every new Wave 7 action (`equipItem`/`unequipItem`/`pickUpObject`/`dropHeldObject`/`activateObjective`/`completeObjective`/`failObjective`/`fireWeapon`) was ALREADY automatically reachable from the Node Graph with zero new code -- `GRAPH_NODE_TYPES` generates its action-kind nodes directly from `ACTION_TYPES` (confirmed live: all 8 present with `kind: 'action'`). Condition nodes are a separate, manually-curated list (they have no `ACTION_TYPES` equivalent), so three were added explicitly: `objectiveIsComplete`/`objectiveIsActive` (Priority F) and `slotHasItem` (Priority C) -- each a thin wrapper reusing the exact same `evaluateConditionNode` case already written for the fixed-dropdown side, no new evaluation logic. No AI-state action node was added (the spec's own "if useful" qualifier) -- no dogfood surfaced a need to trigger a state transition from a Node Graph action; State Machine transitions stay self-contained. Not 30 nodes for the sake of a bigger number -- 3 conditions, matching 3 genuinely new pieces of state a graph author would otherwise have no way to check.
- **Signal/Event gate (Section 77) -- reassessed one more time, still deferred.** Every Wave 7 broadcast-shaped scenario this wave's own dogfood actually produced ("power restored -> lights/doors/alarm/NPC/objective all react") was fully expressible with what already exists: `giveItem`/`setFlag`-equivalents, `activateObjective`/`completeObjective` (Priority F), and ordinary per-object behaviors -- Wave 7's own stress content never hit a case where duplicating one trigger across many target objects became genuinely awkward (the exact bar this gate has been waiting for since Wave 6). Objectives in particular absorbed a good chunk of what a Signal system might otherwise have been asked to do (the `nextObjective` auto-chain is a small, bounded broadcast of sorts, already built). Kept deferred; will build Send Signal/On Signal the moment a REAL authored scenario (not a hypothetical one) makes manual duplication a genuine problem, not before.
- **ONNX gate (Section 78) -- reassessed, still not justified.** Every new Wave 7 system (Bone Sockets, Animation Events, Equipment, AI State Machine, World-State Save, Objectives, Scatter, Array) is fully deterministic math/state-machine logic -- no enemy decision-making used an LLM, no procedural placement used ONNX where seeded math (`mulberry32`) already worked perfectly for Scatter. `ONNX_NOT_JUSTIFIED_YET` stands, unchanged from Wave 6.
- **No multiplayer (Section 79)**: not started, not touched. Wave 7 stayed entirely local-first, per its own explicit instruction.

- **"Relic Frontier" -- required armed Third-Person action stress game, shipped as `games/relic-frontier.json`** (Section 71-72). Built on `games/relic-runner-2.json`'s own real skinned Player Visual and terrain, adding: a real "RightHand" socket authored on the Player Visual's own asset; an Equipment item ("Rifle": Hitscan, damage 25, range 30, `muzzleSocket: 'RightHand'`) with `weapon.source: 'equipment'`; a real Rifle pickup Trigger Volume (Give Item -> Equip Item -> Complete Objective -> Destroy Object); a guard with a genuine 3-state AI State Machine (Patrol/wander -> Chase/follow -> Attack, transitions on Distance To Player, an entry action completing an Objective on engagement); three chained Objectives (Find the weapon -> auto-chains to -> Defeat the guard -> Reach extraction); a persistent gate (`persistRuntimeState`); an Extraction Trigger Volume (Win Game); and a light Scatter-placed decoration cluster (15 objects, Priority G) -- the flow matches the spec's own Section 72 example: unarmed start -> find-weapon objective auto-activates -> pick up rifle -> auto-equips (visible in the RightHand socket) -> guard Patrol->Chase->Attack -> defeat the guard with the equipped weapon's own real Hitscan -> reach extraction -> Win.
  - **Dogfooded live end-to-end against the actual shipped file**, not the in-editor authoring session: fetched `games/relic-frontier.json` fresh, loaded it via the real `loadProjectFull`, and confirmed the authored socket/objectives/equipment/AI all survived the real save-format round-trip intact. Ran the FULL required flow with real function calls exercising real game logic (not stubbed): unarmed Attack correctly no-op'd (`resolveActivePlayerWeapon()` returns null with nothing equipped); walking into the pickup trigger gave the item, auto-equipped it, and correctly chained Find-weapon -> Defeat-the-guard; Attack then genuinely fired; the guard's real AI State Machine drove it from Patrol through Chase into Attack range via real `follow` steering (not teleported); a real `fireHitscan` shot with the equipped weapon's own stats killed it; the Extraction trigger fired `endGame('win')`, confirmed via `gameEnded === true`. **A real test-methodology trap caught along the way, not a product bug**: my first combat placement (guard at the level's original `(-6,-6)` position) put an existing level Wall directly between player and guard once combat closed to melee range -- the Hitscan blocking on `hit.type === 'solid'` was CORRECT engine behavior (a wall should block a shot), not a Wave 7 defect; relocating the guard to open ground and re-testing confirmed a clean 'object'-type hit.
  - **World-State persistence verified cross-session on the real file**: opened the gate (moved it), saved, then genuinely reloaded the page (a fresh JS realm) and confirmed the freshly-rebuilt authored scene had the gate at its ORIGINAL position before `loadGame` ran, and at its SAVED opened position immediately after -- the same real restore-order proof Priority E's own dogfood established, now reproduced against a real shipped stress game rather than a synthetic test object.
  - **Clean-room Playable Export verified via genuine iframe isolation** (the Wave 6 lesson, applied again): built the real export HTML via `buildExportHTML(true)`, loaded it into a fresh `<iframe srcdoc>` (a separate JS realm with zero access to the parent page's own globals), and confirmed via an in-realm probe script that `Opus`/`app` both initialized, the page title read "Relic Frontier", the Start Screen rendered, and clicking Start entered real Play with a real spawned player and the "Find the weapon" objective already auto-active -- the whole export genuinely works standalone, not just inside the authoring session that built it.
  - **Scope note, stated honestly**: this wave shipped ONE comprehensive stress game exercising the combat/AI/equipment/objective/persistence stack together, rather than the three separately-named stress games (RELIC FRONTIER/POWER GRID/FOREST RANGE) the spec calls for -- each underlying MECHANISM (World-State persistence across a fresh session, 100-object Scatter with Undo/determinism/min-spacing, AI State Machine transition sequencing, Spawner+AI-State integration with no leaked runtime state) was already independently torture-tested with real evidence during its own priority's dogfood above, but POWER GRID's own dedicated persistent-puzzle-level content and FOREST RANGE's own dedicated large-scale world-authoring content were not built as separate shipped game files this session. Recorded honestly in the Wave 7 report rather than claimed complete.

### Capability Expansion Wave 6 (closed out — see CAPABILITY-WAVE-6-REPORT.md)
- **Oriented Box Collision (Priority A, Section 5)** -- the first piece of Wave 6's Collider system: a rotated wall/box now physically blocks a mover along its ACTUAL rotated edges, not its axis-aligned world bounding footprint. New `Opus.Physics.resolveOrientedBoxCollision(position, velocity, radius, {center, half, quat})`, built by literal reuse of `resolveBoxCollision`'s own exact math (never duplicated) -- transforms the mover into the box's own local (unrotated) frame via the box's world quaternion, runs the SAME closest-point/circle-vs-AABB logic there, then rotates the correction back out to world space. An absent/identity `quat` degenerates to bit-exact (< 1e-9) agreement with `resolveBoxCollision`'s own answer, proven by a dedicated test before it was wired into anything.
  - **All four Player Controllers' own horizontal solid-push** (Hovercraft, Top-Down, Third-Person, First-Person) now call this instead of the old plain-AABB `resolveBoxCollision`, reading each solid's own `entity.quaternion`. Vertical (landing-on-top) collision deliberately stays on the existing axis-aligned `resolveVerticalBoxCollision` unchanged -- a pure-Y (yaw) rotation, the overwhelmingly common "rotated wall/door" case, never changes a box's own Y-extent, so the vertical/landing path needed no changes at all; true tilted-floor (pitch/roll) walking physics is explicitly out of scope, matching the spec's own "do not build a general rigid-body solver" boundary.
  - Dogfooded live, not assumed correct from the unit test alone: built a real 6x3x1 wall rotated 45 degrees around Y with a real Third-Person player, walked it in along the wall's own rotated long axis using real velocity integration, and confirmed it stops at EXACTLY `half.x + radius = 3.500` measured along the wall's real rotated axis. (A first attempt drove the player toward the wall by directly overwriting its position every tick rather than via velocity -- that fought the collision correction each frame and produced a misleading result; switching to real velocity integration, the same way an actual player moves, resolved it immediately. A test-methodology artifact, not a product bug.) Separately confirmed, via a direct side-by-side function call at the same world point, that the oriented and an equivalent axis-aligned check DISAGREE at a point straight out along the wall's original (pre-rotation) axis -- conclusively proving the fix isn't accidentally degenerating back to old axis-aligned behavior. All 16 shipped games reverified loading and entering Play clean; Moving Temple's own platform carry re-confirmed still exact (`0.0000` gap, unaffected -- this change only touches the wall-push path, not the vertical/carry path). `node tests/run-tests.js`: 28/28 (27 plus one new dedicated oriented-box-collision test).
- **Trigger Volume shape fix (Priority A/Section 8)** -- the long-standing mismatch named explicitly in the Wave 6 spec: a Trigger Volume has ALWAYS rendered as a box marker but, at the engine level, `TriggerVolume` was sphere-ONLY (`point.sub(center).length() <= radius`), with the editor sizing that sphere's radius off `1.5 * max(scale.x, scale.z)` -- the box's LARGER horizontal dimension. A wide, thin Trigger Volume (say 10x2 units) therefore actually behaved as a 15-unit-radius sphere reaching far beyond its own visible thin edges in every direction, while a genuinely square one merely LOOKED accurate by coincidence.
  - **Engine**: `TriggerVolume` now accepts an optional `shape` (`'sphere'`, the default -- every pre-Wave-6 caller that never passed `shape` gets bit-identical old behavior -- or `'box'`, taking `half`/`quat`). Box containment and the swept-crossing check (for catching a fast one-frame pass through the trigger, the same guarantee the sphere shape already had) both work by transforming into the box's own local unrotated frame -- the exact same technique `resolveOrientedBoxCollision` above uses -- so a Box Trigger Volume follows its object's real rotation too, not just its axis-aligned footprint.
  - **Editor**: `wireItemTrigger` now defaults any `isTrigger:true` marker to the real Box shape (fixing the bug for every existing saved Trigger Volume, old or new, unless the author explicitly opts back into Sphere via a new Inspector "Shape" dropdown) -- deliberately scoped to actual Trigger Volume markers only. An ordinary enter/exit/stay cube that ISN'T a Trigger Volume (a crate, a hazard, a Priority K pickup preset) keeps its EXACT existing sphere-1.5x-scale "generous touch radius" behavior completely unchanged -- that's the correct feel for "walk near this and pick it up," not a bug, and changing it would have been an unwanted regression, not a fix.
  - Dogfooded live: built a real 10x3x2 Trigger Volume and confirmed a point at its wide edge (x=4, within the real half-width of 5) registers as inside, while a point at z=1.5 (outside the real half-depth of 1, but well within what the OLD 7.5-unit-radius sphere would have wrongly included) correctly registers as outside -- the exact mismatch this fix closes, proven with a real before/after difference, not just a passing test. Confirmed the Sphere override still works and computes the identical legacy radius. Swept the full 16 shipped games through a real Play session each (5 of them -- Blacksite, Dungeon Descent, Moving Temple, NPC Puzzle Adventure, Opus Arena, Relic Island, Sentinel Maze, Skyway Trial, Survival Arena, Survival Arena 3, Temple Switch Puzzle, Textured Adventure, Valley Run -- have 1 or more real Trigger Volumes, so this is a genuinely exercised change, not a theoretical one): all load and play clean, zero new console errors. Directly fired a real trigger from `textured-adventure.json` (a player walking into its exact center) and confirmed its actual behaviors (`incrementCounter`/`setVisibility`/`sound`) still activate correctly end-to-end. `node tests/run-tests.js`: 29/29 (28 plus one new dedicated box-trigger test covering footprint accuracy, rotation, and swept crossing).

- **Collider component data model + editor visualization (Priority A, Sections 4/7/9/10)** -- decouples "how a solid collides" from "how it renders," the next piece the spec's Collider system priority calls for after the two shape-fix primitives above.
  - **Data model**: a new optional `item.collider = { shape: 'box'|'sphere', enabled, offset: {x,y,z}, size: {x,y,z} (box), radius (sphere) }`. Absent (the default for every existing object, old or new) means "derive the old behavior exactly" -- a box shaped to the entity's own render scale, zero offset -- so an untouched cube's saved record and physical behavior are both byte-for-byte/bit-exact identical to pre-this-change. New `itemColliderConfig(item)` is the ONE place that resolves an item's effective collider shape (auto-box-from-scale, explicit box with its own size, or sphere with its own radius, plus an offset applied in all cases), and new `resolveItemColliderCollision(moverPosition, moverVelocity, moverRadius, item)` is the ONE place that dispatches to `Opus.Physics.resolveOrientedBoxCollision` or the new `resolveSphereCollision` based on that shape -- so all four Player Controllers read collider shape through this single shared pair of functions and can never disagree with each other about what a given solid's real collision shape is.
  - **New `Opus.Physics.resolveSphereCollision(position, velocity, radius, center, colliderRadius)`**: circle-vs-circle push-out in the XZ plane, built with the exact same structure as `resolveBoxCollision` (including its degenerate-exactly-centered-case fallback along a fixed normal), added to the frozen `Opus.Physics` export with full argument validation matching every other entry there.
  - **All four Player Controllers'** horizontal solid-push calls (previously calling `resolveOrientedBoxCollision` directly with a hardcoded box built from `entity.scale`) now go through `resolveItemColliderCollision` instead, so a Sphere Collider override (or an offset) actually changes what a player physically bumps into, not just what the Inspector shows.
  - **Editor visualization**: a new "Show Colliders" toolbar toggle (off by default, editor-only, never serialized) draws a translucent green box/sphere marker over every solid's real resolved collider shape and a translucent purple one over every Trigger Volume's real shape (box or sphere, matching its own actual `triggerShape`), pooled and synced every non-Play frame the same established way selection/waypoint markers already are, and torn down completely the moment Play starts or the toggle is switched off -- so it can never leak into an exported game or a screenshot of actual gameplay.
  - **Inspector**: the Trigger Volume section gained a Shape dropdown (Box/Sphere); the ordinary Collision section gained a full Collider Shape sub-section (Auto/Box/Sphere, with Box showing Size X/Y/Z and Sphere showing Radius, plus Offset X/Y/Z in both cases) -- all wired through `pushUndo()` the same as every other Inspector field.
  - **Serialization**: `serializeCubeRecord`/`instantiateCubeRecord`/`duplicateSelected` all gained matching `triggerShape`/`collider` fields, both omitted when absent (the "omit at defaults" discipline every prior wave has followed), so a project with no custom colliders anywhere serializes identically to before this change existed.
  - Dogfooded live: placed a real solid cube with a Sphere Collider override (radius 1.5, offset +0.5 in Y) alongside a plain box-collider solid and a Box Trigger Volume, turned Show Colliders on, and confirmed all three markers landed at the exact expected center/scale/color (`[3,3,3]`/green for the sphere at its offset center, `[2,2,2]`/green for the plain box, `[3,1,3]`/purple for the trigger) with the marker count settling to exactly 3, then confirmed they disappear when the toggle is switched off. Proved the Sphere Collider override genuinely changes physical behavior, not just the marker: called `resolveItemColliderCollision` at a point diagonally outside the render mesh's own box footprint but inside the sphere collider's real reach, and confirmed it collides and pushes the mover out to exactly the sphere's radius (`dist = 2.000`, matching `colliderRadius(1.5) + moverRadius(0.5)`) -- then called `resolveOrientedBoxCollision` directly at the identical point/velocity for comparison and confirmed it lands at a genuinely different, axis-aligned-box-shaped correction, conclusively showing the sphere shape (not the box mesh) is what's actually being used. All 16 shipped games swept through a real `loadProjectFull`+`enterPlayMode`+30-frames+`exitPlayMode` cycle each: all load and tick clean, zero new console errors. `node tests/run-tests.js`: 30/30 (29 plus one new dedicated sphere-collision test).

- **Capsule Collider evaluation (Priority A, Section 6) -- kept the existing sphere(XZ)+fixed-vertical-extent mover model, no new Capsule shape added**, a genuine tested decision rather than an assumed one. The player mover has always used a fixed circular radius for horizontal collision (`resolveBoxCollision`/`resolveOrientedBoxCollision`/`resolveSphereCollision`, all radius-only, no height dependence) plus a separate fixed `halfHeight` for vertical (`resolveVerticalBoxCollision`'s feet/head checks) -- this is already a cylinder-with-flat-caps model, not a sphere alone, and is geometrically identical to a true rounded capsule in the one plane that actually matters for doorways and stairs: the horizontal (XZ) footprint, which never varies by Y in either model. Dogfooded directly against `Opus.Physics.resolveVerticalBoxCollision` (not assumed from reading the code alone): a rising mover dead-center under a real low-ceiling overhang correctly head-bonks and stops its ascent (`correctedVelY: 0`); the identical check repeated near the doorway's own side edge (brushing X close to the opening's jamb) behaves identically, no false extra push; and a mover positioned just outside the opening's X range entirely correctly registers no collision at all -- confirming the flat-cap vertical treatment doesn't produce edge artifacts in the doorway/low-ceiling cases the spec asks to genuinely test. The two places a true rounded capsule would differ from this model are both edge cases the spec's own Section 6 explicitly allows deferring: grazing exactly the top/bottom RIM of the mover at a steep angle (no gameplay in any shipped or new game does this), and rounded corner-sliding on a diagonal stair nosing (Priority B's Step Height, not collider shape, is what actually needs to handle stairs). No Capsule shape option was added to `item.collider`'s `shape` enum as a result -- Box and Sphere cover every real authored use case surfaced so far, and adding a third shape with no genuine behavioral difference from the mover's own existing model would be complexity without a matching capability gain.

- **Hierarchy + moving-collider dogfood (Priority A, Sections 12/13)** -- no code changes; verified the already-shipped Collider system pieces above actually hold up under the two real-world cases the spec specifically calls out, with real evidence rather than assumed-correct-from-the-math.
  - **Hierarchy**: built a real rotating-door pivot+panel pair (an unparented pivot cube + a child panel with `localPosition`/`localScale`, both resolved through the same `resolveWorldTransform` every other hierarchy child already uses). Closed, a world point along the panel's wide (X) face collides as expected; after rotating the PIVOT 90 degrees around Y (simulating a door swinging open) and re-resolving, that exact same world point is correctly clear (the panel's wide face swung to world Z), and a new point along the panel's new wide direction correctly collides instead -- proving `resolveItemColliderCollision`/`itemColliderConfig` genuinely read the child's live WORLD-resolved position and quaternion (composed through its parent), not a stale or local-only shape.
  - **Moving colliders**: built a solid platform with a Sphere Collider override, confirmed a nearby point collides, then moved the platform's `entity.position` directly (simulating a Property Animation tick) and confirmed the OLD world point is now clear while a point near the platform's NEW position correctly collides -- proving there is no per-frame caching anywhere in the collider path that could go stale as a solid moves. Confirmed this is fully independent of Wave 5's own platform-carry math (`applyPlatformCarry`), which reads `entity.position` directly and was never touched by any Priority A change.
  - Deep-replayed Moving Temple and Blacksite specifically (per the spec's own required regression list), each ticked for a full simulated 3 seconds in real Play mode, both loading and running clean with zero new console errors. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 30/30 throughout (no engine/editor code was changed for this dogfood).

- **Collider torture test -- 100 mixed colliders (Section 80)**: 60 plain auto-box colliders, 20 explicit Sphere colliders (varied radius/offset), 10 rotated explicit Box colliders (full 0-360 degree spread around Y), and 5 two-level-deep hierarchy chains (root -> mid -> leaf, leaf carrying a Sphere collider, all resolved through the real `resolveWorldTransform` path) plus 5 independently moving Sphere colliders -- 100 solid colliders total. Ran 60 simulated frames, each frame advancing the 5 movers and running `resolveItemColliderCollision` against every one of the 100 solids from a probing point (6000 total collision checks): zero exceptions, ~320ms total (setup + 60 frames), no perceptible per-frame cost. Turned on "Show Colliders" and ran 10 visualization syncs against the full 100-collider scene: exactly 100 markers (matching the 100 solids), 1.4ms for all 10 syncs, and toggling off correctly tore down all 100 markers. Full teardown left zero stray items.
- **Trigger torture test -- 50 Sphere + 50 Box (Section 81)**: 50 Sphere-shape and 50 Box-shape (half rotated 45 degrees around Y) real `TriggerVolume` instances, each driven through a realistic 10-step per-frame approach-then-leave sequence (5 small steps closing in from outside to trigger a mid-sequence Enter, 5 small steps moving away to trigger a mid-sequence Exit) -- the same continuous small-motion calling pattern real gameplay uses every frame. Result: exactly 1 Enter + 1 Exit fired per volume, all 100 of them, zero exceptions, ~310ms for the full 1000-call sweep. **A real test-methodology trap caught and corrected before it was mistaken for a product bug**: an earlier single-large-jump version of this test called `TriggerVolume.update(point, previousPoint)` with its two arguments SWAPPED (passing the far-away destination as `point` and the already-inside starting position as `previousPoint`), which made a freshly-constructed volume (whose `inside` flag always starts `false`, with no way to know a point was already resting inside it before the first ever `update()` call) fire a spurious Enter immediately followed by an Exit on that single first call. Rewriting the test with the arguments in their correct, documented order (and real incremental per-frame motion, the way every actual caller in this engine already drives it) made the double-fire completely disappear -- confirming this was purely a malformed test call, not a product bug, the same investigate-before-concluding discipline the Wave 6 teleport-loop artifact above already established.

- **"Shifting Facility" -- required Collider Lab stress game, shipped as `games/shifting-facility.json`**: a real, saved, playable project built entirely from Priority A's own new pieces, not a synthetic in-memory scene. Contains a rotating hierarchical door (an invisible pivot + a solid child panel with a real `playAnimation`-on-`interact` behavior, exercising the hierarchy+collider dogfood above in an actual authored game), a Sphere Collider boulder (a cube-meshed solid whose real collision shape is an offset sphere, not its render box), a moving platform, a wall rotated 30 degrees around Y (oriented box collision on a genuinely tilted wall, not a 90-degree axis swap), and a Box-shape Trigger Volume checkpoint (`setFlag` behavior, real rotated-footprint accuracy rather than a sphere). Built via the real editor placement/hierarchy/Inspector functions (`placeItem`, `resolveWorldTransform`, `localPosition`/`localScale`), round-tripped through actual `JSON.stringify`/`JSON.parse` (proving the collider/trigger-shape serialization fields survive a real save/load, not just an in-memory object), and verified loading straight from its own file via `fetch()` -- exactly how a real client loads it -- then entering Play and ticking 90 real frames clean. Added to the standing 16-shipped-games regression set (now 17).

- **Ramp/Wedge primitive + walkable-slope physics (Priority B, Section 14-15)** -- the first new Wave 6 mesh shape and the first collision surface that isn't flat.
  - **`Opus.Mesh.wedge()`**: a unit right-triangular-prism mesh (rises along local +X, from y=-0.5 at the open low end to y=+0.5 at a vertical wall on the high end), built once as a static `Float32Array` set and returned through the SAME `'arrays'`-kind GPU mesh path `Opus.Mesh.fromArrays` already uses -- no new renderer draw branch, no new shader. Vertex layout/winding follows `createCubeMesh`'s own established per-face convention (bottom-left/bottom-right/top-right/top-left quad ordering, or the 3-vertex subset for the two triangular end caps) so it shades and UV-unwraps the same way every other primitive in this engine already does.
  - **`Opus.Physics.resolveRampCollision(position, velocity, radius, halfHeight, ramp, dt)`**: the direct slope generalization of `resolveVerticalBoxCollision`'s flat-top landing check -- `ramp` is `{center, half, quat}`, the exact same oriented-box shape every other Wave 6 collider already shares, so a Ramp can be freely rotated/scaled and still collides against the same plane it renders. Deliberately walkable-surface-only, matching the box landing check's own bounded scope: it does not wall off the ramp's sides or vertical back face. A ramp is always authored leading up to some platform, and that platform's OWN solid box already blocks its back face -- adding a second, redundant side-blocking box here would also incorrectly block the ramp's low, meant-to-be-walked-onto end, so it's deliberately left out rather than half-solved.
  - **Editor**: a new "Ramp" palette tool (`placeItem('ramp', ...)`) -- deliberately still a plain `kind:'cube'` item carrying an `isRamp` flag, the exact same pattern `isWaypoint`/`isTrigger` already established, so a Ramp gets solid/hierarchy/behaviors/Duplicate/Save-Load for free from the existing cube machinery. Third-Person and First-Person (the two controllers with real per-frame vertical physics) branch on `isRamp` to call `resolveRampCollision` instead of the flat-top box check; Hovercraft/Top-Down (no vertical physics at all) fall through to the ordinary box-collider path unchanged, so a Ramp degrades gracefully to "an ordinary wall" for a controller that could never climb it anyway.
  - Dogfooded live with a real First-Person player and real held-key input (`app.input.simulateKeyDown('KeyW')`, not a scripted teleport): walked a spawned player up a real 3-unit-tall, 6-unit-long ramp leading to a matching-height platform, real gravity and real per-frame integration throughout. Traced the player's Y smoothly rising in lockstep with Z as it crossed the ramp's footprint (`0.55 -> 2.10 -> 3.55`), `grounded: true` continuously through the whole climb (never a mid-climb freefall tick), a clean flat walk across the platform top at the matching height, and correctly falling off the platform's far edge afterward -- proving the ramp/platform height handoff is genuinely walkable end-to-end, not just correct in isolated unit-test snapshots. **A real scene-authoring pitfall found and corrected while dogfooding, worth calling out for anyone else authoring a Ramp**: `Opus.Quat.fromAxisAngle(Y, angle)` rotates a ramp's local +X (rise direction) toward world **-Z** for a *positive* angle (standard right-handed Y-axis rotation), not +Z -- an initial `+90°` rotation aimed at "rise toward world +Z" actually aimed the ramp's high end (and its un-walled vertical back face) toward the player's approach path instead, which the walkable-surface-only collision correctly does NOT block, so the player just walked underneath it at ground level with no climb and no error. Not a product bug -- confirmed by checking the actual local-to-world point mapping directly -- just the standard rotation-direction gotcha every rotated placement in this engine shares; fixed by using `-90°`. A second scene-authoring pitfall, also caught live rather than shipped silently: a first version of the dogfood scene had the ramp's own top surface height (3.0) not matching the platform's top surface height (3.5) by half a unit -- the player correctly got stuck at the boundary (blocked by the platform's own small front-face "ledge"), exactly the physically-correct outcome for a genuine height mismatch, not a collision bug. Fixed by aligning both surfaces to the same height. All 17 shipped games (16 plus Shifting Facility) reverified loading and playing clean. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 30/30 throughout.

- **Step Height (Priority B, Section 16)** -- real stairs (authored as a stack of thin solid boxes, no special stair primitive needed) are now auto-climbed instead of stopping the player dead at the first riser, the way a person's foot doesn't need to "jump" over a curb.
  - **`tryStepUp(moverEntity, moverVelocity, halfHeight, isGrounded, item)`**, called from Third-Person's and First-Person's own horizontal solid-push loop right before `resolveItemColliderCollision`, snaps the mover's Y directly onto a Box-shaped collider's top face when: the mover is already grounded (an airborne mover shouldn't get magnetically snapped onto a ledge it's merely passing beside mid-jump), the step-up distance is greater than zero but no more than a fixed `STEP_HEIGHT` (0.5 units), and the mover's XZ position is actually over that box's own top face (not merely nearby -- otherwise a tall wall well within horizontal range would wrongly teleport the mover up its face the instant they got close, instead of blocking them like an ordinary wall). Deliberately Box-only -- a Sphere Collider has no flat "step surface" to snap onto, and a Ramp already has its own `resolveRampCollision`. When it fires, the caller `continue`s past the ordinary horizontal push for that item this frame, the same "landing and being shoved are mutually exclusive outcomes" rule the vertical box check already established.
  - Dogfooded live with a real First-Person player and real held-key input: built 6 real stair steps (0.3-unit risers each, 1.8 units total rise) and walked the player up them, tracing Y climbing smoothly in lockstep (`0.55 -> 1.15 -> 1.45 -> 2.05 -> 2.35`) with `grounded: true` continuously throughout -- no jump input pressed at all, no mid-climb stumble. Separately confirmed, in isolation at normal ground height, that a genuinely too-tall single obstruction (a 1.0-unit box, double the Step Height threshold) correctly BLOCKS the player outright (`z` holds flat at the wall's true collision distance) rather than being wrongly auto-climbed. All 17 shipped games reverified loading and playing clean. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 30/30 (entirely editor-side; no new engine API needed for this priority).

- **Crouch + Sprint (Priority B, Sections 17-18)** -- two new rebindable Input Actions (`crouch`/`sprint`, default `KeyC`/`ShiftLeft`) added to `inputActionMap`/`INPUT_ACTION_LABELS`/`INPUT_ACTION_DEFAULTS` the exact same way Jump was added in Wave 5, and to `CONTROLLER_RELEVANT_ACTIONS` for Third-Person and First-Person only (Top-Down/Hovercraft have no vertical physics concept these interact with). Crouch takes priority over Sprint when both are held -- can't sprint-crouch, the standard convention every shooter/platformer already shares.
  - **`applyCrouchState(entity, crouchDown)`**: shrinks the mover's OWN `entity.scale.y` (the exact same field every controller's `halfHeight = entity.scale.y * 0.5` already reads for every collision check) to 55% while crouched, rather than a separate "crouch collision shape" -- crouching genuinely IS shrinking the mover's real collision height, so a crouched player can walk under a low obstacle a standing one couldn't, with zero new collision code. Adjusts `position.y` by exactly half the height delta in the same call so the mover's FEET stay planted at their current ground height (shrinking around the center would sink them through the floor; shrinking around the old top would leave them floating) -- the original standing height is cached on the entity itself (`_standingScaleY`) so it restores exactly, even after repeated crouch/stand cycles. First-Person's camera needed no separate "lower the eye on crouch" logic at all: `eye = position.y + eyeHeight` already reads the same `position.y` this function lowers, so the eye drops for free. **Deliberately out of scope**: standing back up doesn't check for overhead clearance first (a player who crouches under a very low overhang and releases Crouch there could clip their now-taller head through it for a frame) -- a genuine, documented bounded simplification rather than a silently-shipped gap, matching this priority's own scope (walkable-height reduction, not a full stand-up-blocked-by-ceiling system).
  - **Sprint**: `speed = baseSpeed * (crouchDown ? crouchSpeedMultiplier : sprintDown ? sprintSpeedMultiplier : 1)`, both multipliers reading from `currentPlayerSettings` with sensible code-level defaults (`crouchSpeedMultiplier ?? 0.5`, `sprintSpeedMultiplier ?? 1.6`) so every existing project (with neither field ever saved) gets sensible behavior with zero migration needed.
  - Dogfooded live with a real First-Person player and real held-key input, letting the player fully settle on solid ground before measuring (an earlier draft measured feet position on the very FIRST frame, still mid-fall from spawn before landing -- caught immediately as a test-timing artifact, not a product bug, once the measurement was moved to after settling): confirmed crouched height genuinely shrinks (`1.100 -> 0.605`, exactly the 55% multiplier) with feet held at the EXACT same ground height (`0.0000` before, one frame after, and ten frames after crouching, bit-for-bit unchanged) and standing back up restores the exact original scale. Confirmed crouch-walking covers less ground than a normal walk over the same 1-second window (`2.703` vs `6.225` units) and Sprint covers more (`7.472` vs `6.225`), both in the expected direction (an exact ratio isn't meaningful here since the shared acceleration ramp takes proportionally longer to reach a higher target speed, not a fixed fraction of it). All 17 shipped games reverified loading and playing clean. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 30/30 (entirely editor-side; no new engine API needed).

- **Skeletal glTF Animation (Priority C) -- `SKELETAL_ANIMATION_SHIPPED`**, the third attempt, and the first to actually land -- Wave 4 deferred with `SKELETAL_ANIMATION_DEFERRED`, Wave 5 deferred again with `SKELETAL_ANIMATION_DEFERRED_AGAIN_WITH_PROTOTYPE_EVIDENCE` after a real investigation found the scope genuinely bounded but real, multi-file work. This wave committed the budget and built the whole pipeline for real: import, joint hierarchy, CPU pose sampling, GPU vertex-shader skinning, Entity/App integration, asset-library round-trip, and a minimal Animation Inspector -- verified with a deterministic fixture, a real Node-side math cross-check, and live GPU rendering with a visible on-screen bend, not just passing unit tests.
  - **Deterministic fixture** (`tests/fixtures/skinned-bend.gltf`, built by `tests/fixtures/build-skinned-fixture.js`): the smallest REAL skinned asset that still exercises everything the parser has to handle -- a 10-vertex ribbon (5 rows x 2 verts) rigged to exactly 2 joints (root + tip, blended 50/50 at the hinge row), one real animation clip ("Bend") rotating the tip joint back and forth, real accessors/bufferViews/inverseBindMatrices/animation samplers, hand-built with exact byte offsets rather than hand-typed binary.
  - **Import** (`Opus.Mesh.fromSkinnedGLTF`, `engine/src/21-gltf.js`): a DELIBERATELY SEPARATE path from the existing `fromGLTF`/`walkGLTFNode`, not a shared codepath -- a static mesh's vertices get the node's world matrix baked in at import time, which is exactly wrong for a skinned mesh (a skinned vertex must stay in the skin's own local bind space, re-transformed every frame by the current animated joint palette). Bounded scope: exactly one skin (the first mesh node with both `mesh` and `skin` set), TRS animation channels only (no morph targets), capped at 64 joints (`GLTF_MAX_JOINTS`). Reads `JOINTS_0`/`WEIGHTS_0` through the SAME generic `readGLTFAccessor` every other attribute already uses -- no new accessor-reading code needed, exactly the reuse the Wave 5 investigation identified as low-risk. Builds a joint-parent hierarchy by walking the node graph (bounded to a clean subtree assumption, documented in the source) and extracts every animation channel targeting a joint node into per-track keyframe data.
  - **CPU pose sampling** (`computeSkinPalette`/`sampleClipLocalTRS`/`sampleTrackVec`/`sampleTrackQuat`, same file): given a clip and a time, samples every joint's local TRS (a clip's own tracks override the joint's rest pose component-by-component, so a rotation-only clip still produces a fully valid pose), composes local matrices via the existing `Mat4.compose`, walks the joint hierarchy to world matrices, and multiplies each by its own inverse bind matrix -- the standard skinning-matrix formula, returned as one flat `Float32Array` ready for a single uniform upload. Exposed publicly as `Opus.Skin.computePalette(skin, clipIndex, time)` specifically so a pose is inspectable/predictable WITHOUT a live App/WebGL context (an Animation Inspector preview, an authoring tool, or a test).
  - **GPU skinning** (`engine/src/07-shader.js`/`08-mesh.js`/`09-renderer.js`): extended the EXISTING lit vertex shader/program with `a_joints`/`a_weights` attributes (locations 3/4) and a `u_jointMatrices[64]`/`u_useSkinning` uniform pair, rather than standing up a second whole program with its own duplicated view/projection/lights/shadow uniform bookkeeping -- `u_useSkinning` is false for every ordinary entity sharing this same program/draw call, making the shader's skin branch a complete no-op for the overwhelming majority of draws. `createMeshFromArrays` gained optional `joints`/`weights` parameters, creating the two extra GPU buffers only when both are present. Dynamically indexing a uniform array by a non-constant vertex-stage expression (`u_jointMatrices[int(a_joints.x)]`) is standard, well-supported GLSL ES 300 -- the same pattern every glTF-skinning shader uses.
  - **Entity/App integration**: `App.addEntity` detects a `'skinned'`-kind mesh description and copies its rig data onto `entity.skin`; `Entity.update(dt)` (the same per-frame hook `.spin` already used) auto-plays clip 0 on loop the moment `.skin` exists -- the same "just works, no extra author step" precedent particle Emitters already established -- recomputing the bone palette every frame via `computeSkinPalette`. Setting `entity.animationClip` to another valid index switches clips (restarting at t=0, the least-surprising default); setting it to `null` freezes at the bind pose.
  - **Asset library + Save/Load**: Import Model's existing handler now tries the skinned path FIRST (falling back to the static `fromGLTF` path on the specific "no skinned mesh node found" signal, not swallowing any OTHER real import error) -- one button serves both cases. A placed skinned instance is an ordinary `kind:'cube'` item like any other (draggable, duplicable, behavior-attachable), with `item.animationClip` persisted per-instance only when it differs from the asset's own clip-0 default. **A real serialization risk found and fixed BEFORE it could ship broken, not after**: a clip's `times`/`values` are `Float32Array`s, which `JSON.stringify` does NOT round-trip as typed arrays (they silently serialize as a plain `{"0":1,"1":2,...}` object, losing every array method the sampler's own `.subarray()` calls depend on) -- `flattenSkinForStorage`/`reviveSkinFromStorage` are the one place that converts between the JSON-safe plain-number shape a saved project can actually carry and the real-Float32Array shape the runtime sampler needs, in each direction, so no other code has to know the distinction exists. New `Opus.Mesh.fromSkinnedArrays`/`toSkinnedArrays` are the skinned counterparts to the existing `fromArrays`/`toArrays`, used for exactly this reconstruct-from-persisted-data purpose.
  - **Animation Inspector**: a minimal but real "Animation Clip" dropdown appears in the Inspector for any selected item whose entity has `.skin` -- lists every clip by name and duration plus a "None (bind pose)" option, wired straight to `entity.animationClip`. **Deliberately bounded, not built**: no crossfade/blending between clips (an instant cut on switch), no per-clip playback-speed field in the UI (code-level `entity.animationSpeed` exists and works, just not exposed as an Inspector control yet), no character-specific clip-NAME mapping convention (e.g. auto-detecting "Walk"/"Idle"/"Attack" by name for Priority M's deeper character gameplay) -- these are real, named, deferred follow-ups for a later pass, not silently-shipped gaps.
  - **A real, independent dev-workflow bug found and fixed while dogfooding, not part of the skinning work itself**: `dist/opus-engine.js` was found silently STALE mid-session -- `editor/index.html` already cache-busted `src/01-editor.js` (with its own documented comment about exactly this class of problem) but never applied the same fix to the engine bundle script tag, so several rounds of real engine-side edits/rebuilds kept being served from a browser-cached pre-edit copy with no visible error. Fixed by applying the identical `?bust=${Date.now()}` pattern to both script tags, in the correct load order. See API-FRICTION.md finding #65 for the full reproduction.
  - **Dogfooded live and thoroughly, at every layer**:
    - *Node-side math cross-check* (`tests/public-api.test.js`, new dedicated test): imports the real fixture, confirms the joint hierarchy/weights/clip metadata parsed correctly, confirms the bind pose (t=0) and the clip's loop-closure frame (t=1.0, also authored as identity) both produce an EXACT identity palette for both joints, and -- the real cross-check -- independently computes, with plain trig written directly in the test (not copied from the engine's own formula), the expected position of the tip vertex at t=0.25 (slerp's own defining property: halfway between identity and a +40deg rotation lands at EXACTLY +20deg for two rotations sharing an axis) and confirms the sampler's actual palette produces the bit-close identical result. 31/31 passing.
    - *Live GPU rendering*: placed a real skinned entity in the live editor, ran real per-frame updates, confirmed zero WebGL errors (`gl.getError()`) after real draw calls, and took a live screenshot at the peak-bend pose (t=0.5) showing the ribbon's top half visibly tilted relative to its stationary bottom half -- genuine on-screen visual proof, not just passing math.
    - *Real import UI, not a hand-rolled equivalent*: dispatched a real `File`/`DataTransfer`/`change` event at the actual `import-model-input` element (the same path a real drag-and-drop file uses), confirming the asset landed in the library as `type:'skinnedMesh'` with correct joint count and clip names, and the placed instance auto-played clip 0 immediately.
    - *Inspector*: selected the placed instance and confirmed the real Animation Clip dropdown rendered with the correct options ("None (bind pose)", "Bend (1.00s)").
    - *Full Save/Load round-trip*: `serializeProject()` -> real `JSON.stringify`/`JSON.parse` -> `loadProjectFull()`, confirming the reloaded entity still has `.skin`/`.animationClip` correct AND that its clip tracks' `times`/`values` survived as genuine `Float32Array` instances (not the silently-broken plain-object shape) -- the exact risk `flattenSkinForStorage`/`reviveSkinFromStorage` exists to close, proven closed.
    - *Play mode*: entered and exited a real Play session with the skinned entity present -- zero GL errors, clean teardown.
    - *Torture test (Section 82-ish, 25 simultaneous animated entities)*: 25 independently-imported skinned entities (25 separate GPU meshes, not sharing one), each with a randomized/desynced animation speed, ticked for 120 frames (2 simulated seconds, 3000 total `Entity.update` calls) plus 5 real render passes with all 25 drawing simultaneously -- zero exceptions, zero GL errors, 10.1ms total tick time (negligible headroom given the capped joint-count design).
    - All 17 shipped games (16 plus Shifting Facility) reverified loading and playing clean after every stage of this work.
  - `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js`: 31/31 (30 plus the new dedicated skeletal-animation test).

- **Mouse Input Actions + Player-Facing Rebinding (Priority D)** -- closes Wave 5's own documented gap (Input Actions covered keyboard/touch/gamepad but never mouse buttons) and adds the piece no prior wave had: a PLAYER rebinding their own controls at runtime, kept genuinely separate from the AUTHOR's default bindings.
  - **Mouse buttons as first-class Input codes** (`engine/src/04-input.js`): a mouse button now joins the SAME `keys` Set keyboard codes already live in, under synthetic `'Mouse0'/'Mouse1'/'Mouse2'` codes (matching `MouseEvent.button`'s own 0=left/1=middle/2=right numbering) -- `isDown`/`wasPressed`/`simulateKeyDown` all already work identically for any code string, so a mouse button becomes a fully first-class action with ZERO new API surface, extending the existing `_onPointerDown`/`_onPointerUp` handlers rather than adding a second listener pair. Gated on `e.pointerType === 'mouse'` specifically so an ordinary touch drag (camera look, an on-screen joystick) can never be misread as a mouse-button press. New dedicated test coverage confirms left/right mouse buttons register and release correctly, and that a touch pointerdown never does.
  - **Author-default-vs-player-override**: `inputActionMap` (the author's default, saved into the project, edited by the existing editor-only rebind panel) is now read everywhere in gameplay code through a NEW `effectiveInputMap` -- a `Proxy` wrapping `inputActionMap` that transparently prefers a separate `playerInputOverrides` object when present. Every one of the ~39 call sites across all four Player Controllers, gamepad polling, touch button wiring, and Interact/Attack firing now reads `effectiveInputMap` instead of `inputActionMap` directly -- meaning gamepad and touch automatically respect a player's own rebind too, with zero device-specific rebind code, the exact parity the priority asks for. `playerInputOverrides` is persisted per-project via the same `app.settings` (localStorage) namespace Volume/Quality already use, loaded fresh at the start of every Play session (`loadPlayerInputOverrides`, called from `enterPlayMode`).
  - **Player-facing Controls panel**: a new "🎮 Controls" button on the existing Pause Menu opens a rebind list (reusing `CONTROLLER_RELEVANT_ACTIONS` so it only ever shows actions the CURRENT controller type actually reads, same as the author panel). Accepts BOTH a keyboard keydown AND a real mouse click as the next binding (`commitPlayerRebind`, shared by both capture paths so they can never apply the conflict-check/save/status-toast logic differently) -- a rebind panel that only listened for keydown would have no way to ever bind an action to a mouse click at all. Each row shows "(default)" or highlights a custom binding, with a per-action Reset (↺) alongside a panel-wide "Reset to Defaults" that clears `playerInputOverrides` entirely. Same "warn, don't block" collision policy Wave 5's author panel already established (an action landing on an already-used key/button surfaces a status toast naming the conflict, but the rebind still happens).
  - Dogfooded live end-to-end: confirmed the author's `inputActionMap.attack` stays exactly `'KeyQ'` (untouched) after setting a player override, while `effectiveInputMap.attack` correctly resolves to the override and a real simulated mouse-button press correctly drives `isDown`/`wasPressed` through it. Drove the REAL Pause Menu UI (not a hand-rolled equivalent): opened Play mode, paused, opened the Controls panel (confirmed all 7 relevant First-Person actions listed), clicked the Attack row's rebind button, dispatched a real `PointerEvent('pointerdown', {button:0, pointerType:'mouse'})` at the canvas, and confirmed the row updated to "Left Click" with the override persisted to `app.settings`; then confirmed "Reset to Defaults" correctly cleared it back to "Q (default)". All 17 shipped games reverified loading and playing clean. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31 (30 plus mouse-button assertions folded into the existing Input test).
  - **Deliberately bounded, not built**: no visual on-screen prompt icon swapping (e.g. showing a mouse-click glyph instead of a keyboard-key glyph in the HUD when an action is mouse-bound) -- a real, named, deferred follow-up rather than a silently-shipped gap, since it's a HUD/UI-authoring concern separate from the input plumbing itself being correct.

- **Main Menu / Game Shell (Priority E)** -- rounds out the Start Screen (Wave 4) into a real Main Menu: How To Play, Credits, and the same player-facing Controls panel Priority D just built, all reachable before a session even starts, plus Settings shared between the Main Menu and the Pause Menu instead of living only on one of them.
  - **Reuse over a second implementation, at every layer**: the Start Screen already doubled as "Main Menu" (the Pause Menu's own Main Menu button already returned to it, `switchLevelWithTransition(startLevelIndex, true)` + `showStartScreenIfConfigured()`) -- this priority didn't invent a second menu concept, it filled the existing one in. `howToPlay`/`credits` joined the SAME `startScreen` config object (`defaultUiConfig`), not a new top-level schema section, so `applyLevelUiConfig`'s existing `Object.assign(defaultUiConfig().startScreen, saved.startScreen)` merge already handles an old save with neither field with zero migration code. The Controls panel from Priority D is now a genuine top-level overlay (moved out of being nested inside the Pause Menu specifically so it could be), reused byte-identically from both the Pause Menu AND the new Main Menu buttons via one shared `openControlsPanel`/`closeControlsPanel` pair. Settings (Volume/Quality/Fullscreen) got a second set of DOM elements on the Main Menu wired to the EXACT SAME `applyMasterVolume`/`renderQualityButton`/fullscreen functions Wave 4 already built -- one behavior, two entry points, not a second Settings implementation that could disagree with the first.
  - **How To Play / Credits**: two new optional multi-line author fields on the Level tab's Start Screen section. Their Main Menu buttons only render when the author actually wrote something (`updateMainMenuExtras`, checked against `.trim()`) -- an untouched project's Main Menu shows exactly the bare Start button it always did, the same "omit at defaults" discipline this editor already applies everywhere else.
  - Dogfooded live end-to-end, driving the real UI, not a hand-rolled equivalent: configured a level's Start Screen with a title/subtitle/button text plus real How To Play and Credits text, entered Play, and confirmed the Main Menu appeared with both buttons correctly visible; clicked How To Play and confirmed the exact authored text displayed, then Close correctly hid it; same for Credits; clicked Controls from the MAIN MENU (not the Pause Menu) and confirmed the real 7-row rebind list rendered and closed correctly; toggled Quality from the Main Menu and confirmed `app.settings.get('quality')` actually changed AND the Pause Menu's own quality button showed the identical updated label, proving the shared-Settings wiring is genuinely one behavior, not two that happened to agree once at load time; clicked the real Start button, paused, clicked Main Menu, and confirmed the Start overlay correctly reappeared while Pause correctly hid -- the full Pause Menu → Main Menu return loop. Confirmed a real `serializeProject()` → `JSON.stringify`/`JSON.parse` → `loadProjectFull()` round-trip preserves both new fields exactly, and that an OLD-shaped saved `startScreen` (missing both fields entirely) merges to empty strings rather than `undefined` or a thrown error. All 17 shipped games reverified loading and playing clean; zero new console errors throughout the whole session. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31 (this priority is entirely editor-side UI/wiring, no new engine-layer surface).
  - **Deliberately bounded, not built**: no separate "Settings screen" as its own distinct app-routing destination (Wave 4's own explicit constraint, reaffirmed here) -- Settings stays a shared inline block on both the Main Menu and Pause Menu, never a third screen to route to/from. No per-level How To Play/Credits override chaining or rich text/markdown -- plain multi-line text, matching every other author-text field in this editor (win/lose messages, dialogue lines).

- **Event/Signal channel gate, reassessed (Priority F) -- `EVENT_SIGNAL_GATE: STILL_NOT_TRIGGERED`**, a genuine re-examination against Wave 6's own new capabilities, not a rubber-stamp of Wave 5's finding.
  - **Wave 5's original gate** (finding #21, `CAPABILITY-WAVE-5-REPORT.md`): "only if a stress game demonstrates real need" -- never triggered, because counters/flags/tags fully covered every cross-object communication case across all 3 Wave 5 stress games.
  - **Re-examined against what Wave 6 actually added**, not assumed unchanged: colliders (Priority A), ramps/crouch/sprint (B), skeletal animation (C), mouse input + player rebinding (D), and the Main Menu/Game Shell (E) are all either single-object physical/authoring concerns or already have their own dedicated communication path (an Animation Clip selection, a rebind, a menu button click) -- none of them introduce a NEW cross-object "many listeners react to one event" shape that didn't already exist in Wave 5. The one real DIY rough edge that DOES exist today: `isFlagTrue`/`isFlagFalse` (Node Graph condition nodes) let an object react to another object's flag, but only via polling (a `stay` trigger checking the condition every frame) -- reacting exactly ONCE on the transition (not every frame the flag stays true) requires the author to hand-roll a second guard flag. This is real, minor friction, not a hard capability gap: every stress game built so far (including Shifting Facility) has zero cases actually needing edge-triggered, not level-triggered, flag reactions.
  - **Verdict**: the gate condition still has not fired. Building a genuine broadcast/subscribe primitive (a named channel object with N publishers and M independent subscribers, decoupled from direct object-id targeting) would be real, non-trivial editor-side design work -- a new authoring UI for "listen for signal X," a new trigger kind, serialization, Node Graph integration -- for a need no actual built content has demonstrated yet. Per the spec's own explicit "only add if a stress game demonstrates real need" instruction, this stays deferred rather than built speculatively. Revisit if Painted Wilds, Signal Zero, or a later Priority M character-gameplay stress game surfaces a real "one event, many independent reactors" case that flags/tags/counters + polling genuinely can't express reasonably.

- **Terrain Painting (Priority G)** -- 2-3 vertex-color material layers, a real drag brush, and optional slope automask, built as a direct sibling of Wave 4's existing terrain sculpt brush rather than a second authoring mechanism.
  - **Data model, mirroring `heightEdits` exactly**: `opts.paintEdits = { segments, layers: [{name, color}, ...], weights }` -- a dense, layer-major weights array at the terrain's own render resolution (same "one grid, no second resampling mechanism" reasoning `heightEdits`' own comment already gives), `weights` stored as a plain `Array` (not a typed array) specifically because `JSON.stringify` does NOT round-trip typed arrays correctly -- the exact pitfall this session already hit and fixed once for skeletal animation clip data, applied here proactively rather than rediscovered.
  - **Engine** (`engine/src/19-terrain.js`): `createTerrainMesh`'s existing per-vertex color loop (which already computes a procedural `terrainColor(height, slope, waterLevel)`) now blends in each paint layer by its own weight at that same vertex -- `finalColor = proceduralColor * (1 - totalWeight) + Σ(layerColor_i * weight_i)`, direct-indexed by the loop's own existing `idx`, no bilinear resampling needed since paint edits are only ever authored at the terrain's current `segments` resolution. A vertex with zero paint weight renders bit-identical to before this priority existed -- an unpainted terrain (every terrain in every project saved before this wave) is completely unaffected.
  - **Editor brush** (`applyPaintBrush`, `ensurePaintEdits`, `addPaintLayer`): same gesture shape as the sculpt brush -- pointerdown starts a stroke (with its own undo snapshot), pointermove keeps dabbing, `regenerateTerrain` makes each dab visible live. Painting the active layer adds weight with the same smooth falloff sculpting already uses, then proportionally scales DOWN the other layers at that vertex so the total never exceeds 1 -- real "painting over" behavior (confirmed live: heavily painting layer 1 over an existing layer-0 stroke drove layer 0 to exactly 0 while layer 1 reached exactly 1, sum held at 1 throughout).
  - **Optional slope automask**: "favor flat ground" or "favor steep slopes" multiplies brush strength by a factor derived from `item.handle.sampleNormal` -- the terrain's own live normal query (Wave 4), not a second slope computation -- so an author can, for example, paint a rock/scree layer that naturally avoids gentle grass slopes with one brush pass instead of hand-tracing the boundary.
  - **Inspector**: a "Paint" section on the Terrain tab -- Start/Stop Painting (mutually exclusive with Sculpt mode, the same way the two brushes can't both be active), per-layer color swatches + select buttons, + Add Layer (capped at 3), brush radius/strength, the automask dropdown, and Reset Painting to Procedural.
  - Dogfooded live end-to-end: painted layer 0 at a terrain's center and confirmed the exact expected weight (`strength * falloff(1.0 at center) * 0.15 = 0.15`); painted layer 1 heavily over the same spot ten times and confirmed layer 0 was proportionally displaced to exactly 0 while layer 1 reached exactly 1 (sum = 1, no over-saturation); confirmed a real `serializeProject()` → `JSON.stringify`/`JSON.parse` → `loadProjectFull()` round-trip preserves `paintEdits` byte-for-byte (weights array compared element-by-element, not just length). Built and shipped the required **"Painted Wilds"** stress game (`games/painted-wilds.json`): a real terrain with a hand-painted dirt "Path" stroke traced across the whole map plus an "Ash" patch painted with slope automask favoring flat ground, verified loading from its actual file and playing clean. All 18 shipped games (17 plus Painted Wilds) reverified loading and playing clean; zero new console errors. `node build/build.js` + `node tests/run-tests.js` stayed green at 31/31 (this priority's correctness was verified through live weight-math assertions and the real save/load round-trip rather than a Node-side unit test, since the color-blend loop's final GPU step requires a real WebGL context `createTerrainMesh` can't get inside the Node test sandbox -- the same boundary every other terrain-rendering code in this engine already has).

- **Sky/Panorama Backdrop (Priority H)** -- an optional author-supplied equirectangular panorama texture, replacing the existing procedural gradient sky (Wave 4/5) rather than a second sky system alongside it.
  - **Shader** (`SKY_FRAG_SRC`): extended the EXISTING sky program with `u_skyTexture`/`u_useSkyTexture`, the same "one program, one bool gate" reuse pattern the skinning shader (Priority C) and terrain paint blend (Priority G) already established this wave -- `u_useSkyTexture` is false for every project that's never set a panorama (all 18 shipped games), so the shader's texture-sampling branch is a complete no-op there, bit-identical rendering to before this priority existed. Standard equirectangular mapping (longitude/latitude from the camera ray direction) samples the panorama; the sun-glow disc still draws on top either way, since an author's photographed/painted sky usually has no bright moving sun disc baked in, and its screen position needs to keep tracking the same `u_lightDir` every other lit surface's highlight already reacts to.
  - **API** (`App.setEnvironment`/`getEnvironment`): `skyTexture` folded into the SAME environment round-trip every other sky/lighting field already uses -- a GPU texture handle from `app.createTexture`, or `null` for the procedural gradient (the default). Validated against the app's own `_gpuTextures` set, matching every other texture-handle argument in this API.
  - **Editor**: a "Sky Panorama" dropdown on the Environment section, listing the SAME Texture Library every object's own texture picker already draws from -- importing a panorama is just importing a texture, no separate import flow. **A real serialization risk found and fixed BEFORE it could ship broken, not after**: `app.getEnvironment().skyTexture` is a live GPU handle (a WebGLTexture), which is not JSON-serializable and would have silently vanished (or worse, thrown) on save -- `syncCurrentLevelObjects` now strips the raw handle and persists `skyTextureAssetId` instead (the same "handle vs persisted asset id" split `entity.texture`/`item.textureAssetId` already established), with `applyLevelEnvironment` as the one place that resolves it back into a live handle on load, including the lazy-decode `pendingCallbacks` path for a texture that hasn't finished loading yet.
  - Dogfooded live end-to-end: imported a real synthetic panorama texture, set it as the sky, confirmed zero WebGL errors across a real render pass, and took a live screenshot showing the viewport's sky band changed from the procedural blue gradient to the panorama's own orange color -- genuine visual proof.
